Glossary

One rendering per controlled Latin term, so that what repeats in the Latin repeats in the Portuguese. Under a term: its word study, and the psalms where a decision turns on it.

One rendering per controlled Latin term, so that what repeats in the Latin repeats in the Portuguese. It grows from the work: add an entry when a word turns out to recur or to collide with a neighbour; put your psalm number beside it. Status: decided (Gustavo) · settled (ruled by Claude overnight under Gustavo's delegation, with reasons in DECISIONS.md — use it; he reviews) · working (use it; nobody has ruled) · open (no settled rendering — propose, and make it a decision in your psalm).

Heavy-duty words get a study in words/ (python3.13 research/psalterium/words.py <name> '<regex>' --greek … --pt …).

Address and grammar

Status
Godvós, lowercase pronounsdecided
plural humansvósdecided Ps 10:2 — a cost of vós found: quómodo dícitis ánimæ meæ (the advisers, plural) in a psalm that turns to God in 10:4 (perfecísti, singular): the Latin tells the two apart by number, Portuguese cannot. como dizeis à minha alma was heard by the blind reader FIRST as said to the Lord; como dizeis vós (the subject named, set against o Senhor of the first colon — D2 allows it) was heard as said to others. The same remedy may serve wherever plural men are addressed next to God (cf. D25 on 7:13). Ps 13:6 consílium ínopis confudístis (the evildoers, plural, between two verses naming the Lord in the third person): the Ps 10:2 remedy used again — vós envergonhastes (the subject named); the blind reader heard it as said to the evildoers, not to God. The remedy holds twice now.
a singular human addresseetudecided Ps 20:9–13 (after 20:8 names the king in the third person, Inveniátur manus tua … Pones eos): the addressee is God or the king, and the Latin leaves it open (20:10 then names Dóminus in the third person). Kept vós — it holds both readings; the blind reader heard the Lord throughout, with the doubt the Latin itself raises at 20:10. tu (the king) is one touch away (decision address). For Gustavo.
God speaking to a single addressee — his Anointed / Son (2:7–9), the king, the just man (90:9–13)tu — address follows the speaker: vós is the voice of the one who prays to God, not a mark of the addressee's rank; when God speaks he says tu (Tu és meu Filho, eu hoje te gerei)settled (D17); was: open — ruled in Ps 2 (decision address), wants confirming; will govern 109:1, 109:4, 88:27–28. Does not settle the psalmist addressing the Messiah-king (Ps 44). The vós form is selectable in Ps 2 (Vós sois meu Filho … Pedi-me)
servíre at the vós imperative (Servíte Dómino, 2:11, 99:2)Sede servos do Senhor — because the bare Servi ao Senhor is also "I served"settled (D18); was: open — wants a ruling like D13: Latinist (twice, minor) and stylist both asked for Servi ao Senhor; the object is a noun, so the enclitic exception does not apply; the run of imperatives around it (entendei … deixai-vos instruir … exultai) makes the mood plain — if the ban may yield to context, this is the verse
possessiveswith the article: o vosso nome, a minha almaworking
vós imperatives equal to a first-person past (ouvi, abri, parti)avoid the bare form; with an enclitic pronoun the imperative stands (Abri-me as portas da justiça, 117:19)decided (Gustavo); the enclitic exception settled (D13)

DóminusPs 1 Ps 2 Ps 3 Ps 5 Ps 6 Ps 7 Ps 8 Ps 9 Ps 10 Ps 11 Ps 12 Ps 13 Ps 14 Ps 15 Ps 16 Ps 17 Ps 18 Ps 19 Ps 20 Ps 21 Ps 22 Ps 23 Ps 24 Ps 25 Ps 26 Ps 27 Ps 28 Ps 29 Ps 30 Ps 31 Ps 32 Ps 33 Ps 34 Ps 35 Ps 36 Ps 37 Ps 38 Ps 39 Ps 41 Ps 53 Ps 117 Ps 118 Ps 133Senhorworkingvocative without ó unless the Latin has o Ps 8:2a = 8:10 Dómine, Dóminus noster → Senhor, nosso Senhor: one word twice, as the Latin and the Greek have it against the Hebrew's two names (MS1932 nosso Dominador soberano and DM1962 nosso Deus both avoid it). Possessive first, so that the second member is heard as the title nosso Senhor in apposition — Portuguese has no case to do what Dómine / Dóminus does; Senhor, Senhor nosso (the Latin's order) is the option, refused because Senhor, Senhor back to back is a doubled cry. The stylist's best line; the Latinist passed it twice; the blind reader did not stop at it.

inténderethe concordance Ps 10 Ps 16 Ps 37atendersettled (D3)stands parallel to exáudi (16:1, 54:2, 60:2, 140:1) Ps 5:3 Atendei à voz da minha oração — the stylist's best line of the psalm; the blind reader heard "grant what I ask" before "listen" (the cost D3 named, in reverse). Ps 16:1a Exáudi … inténde → Escutai … atendei — the first of the four parallels this row names; the two verbs stood two; no reader remarked.
áuribus percíperePs 5 Ps 53dar ouvidos a (dai ouvidos às palavras da minha boca)settled (D4)Ps 53:4 proposes it for all eight places (5:2, 16:1b, 38:13, 48:2, 53:4, 83:9, 85:6, 142:1): keeps the ears, stands apart from escutar / ouvir / atender (it is the second member after exáudi in four of them, and beside inténde in 85:6) and from inclína aurem; works with no object (83:9 dai ouvidos, Deus de Jacó) and at the vós imperative (dai ≠ dei); cost: in everyday speech it leans to "give credence to" — yet the stylist named 53:4 the psalm's best line and the blind reader found nothing to mishear; alternatives prestai ouvidos a (pt-PT 85:6, 142:1), percebei com os ouvidos; MS1932 atender refused (it is inténdere) Ps 5:2, its first place: Dai ouvidos, Senhor, às minhas palavras — holds; natural order (MS1932 has the same words); Latinist clean, stylist and blind reader silent. It stands there beside intéllege → entendei and inténde → atendei, three verbs kept three. Ps 16:1b Áuribus pércipe oratiónem meam → Dai ouvidos à minha oração — a place this row named; D4 held; no reader remarked. Ps 38:13 áuribus pércipe lácrimas meas → dai ouvidos às minhas lágrimas — a place this row named; D4 held; no reader remarked.

justificatiónesPs 118preceitossettled (D15)Ps 118 — words/ps118-terms.md. 29 of its 30 places are in Ps 118 (+104:45); δικαιώματα. justificações means excuses / the act of justifying in Portuguese and needs a footnote; Matos Soares never uses it; Portuguese Bibles have mandamentos e preceitos do Senhor for Luke 1:6 mandátis et justificatiónibus. Live alternatives: decretos (DM1962), justificações. Not the singular 118:62 judícia justificatiónis tuæ (= δικαιοσύνη): decide it there
præcéptumthe concordance Ps 2 Ps 7 Ps 18decretosettled (D19)proposed by Ps 118, which never has the word: 8× (2:6, 7:7b, 18:9, 80:5, 93:20, 98:7, 104:10, 148:6), always singular, πρόσταγμα in six of them; frees preceitos for justificatiónes. If justificatiónes moves to decretos, this returns to preceito. Ps 2:6, the first verse to meet it, rules that decreto holds: proclamando o seu decreto — a king set on Sion proclaims a decree and then quotes it (2:7); neither blind reader nor the Latinist remarked on it. Tried in the other seven places (ps002/prayed.json, decision praeceptum): it fails nowhere; 18:9 o decreto do Senhor é luminoso (the one ἐντολή) is the least natural. Ready to be settled Ps 7:7b, its second place: levantai-vos, Senhor, meu Deus, no decreto que mandastes — holds: a judicial word in a judicial psalm; the Latinist passed it twice, the blind reader heard "rise to carry out / in keeping with the decree"; the stylist asked for preceito, refused under D19. Ps 18:9 præcéptum Dómini lúcidum → o decreto do Senhor é luminoso — the list's third place, held; passed every reader.

DM1962 sentenças is the option in Ps 118; julgamentos refused (rhymes with mandamentos)

verbum / sermo / elóquiumthe concordance the concordance the concordance Ps 5 Ps 11 Ps 17 Ps 18 Ps 21 Ps 28 Ps 32 Ps 90 Ps 118palavra / palavra / o que dissestes in both numbers (before D26 the plural was os vossos ditos) (segundo o que dissestes, guardei o que dissestes). sermo: the main session overruled the agent's fala (D15: one Greek word, the stylist refused falas three times; verses that set the two side by side decide locally). elóquium: the noun o vosso dito failed the stylist six times out of six; the clause was the blind reader's own paraphrase and held on a second stylist pass (D16); where the singular must be a noun (118:123, 103:34, 104:19) the verse decides locally, dito availablesettled (D15, D16, D26)Ps 118 — words/ps118-terms.md. verbum and sermo are one Greek word (λόγος); elóquium is λόγιον. They meet in single verses (55:11, 102:20, 118:42 verbum + sermo; 147:4 elóquium + sermo), so three words. sermo → fala: L&S "talk, ordinary speech"; 54:22 as suas falas são mais macias que o azeite, 138:4 não há fala na minha língua. elóquium → dito: a thing uttered, God's or a man's (18:15a os ditos da minha boca); the least certain of the set — option promessa (DM1962) fits the formula secúndum elóquium tuum but not 118:67, 158, 172 nor a man's elóquia; oráculo refused (proparoxytone at ten cadences). Merging all into palavra is what Douay-Rheims, Matos Soares and DM1962 did. Heard (118:1–32): the stylist refused falas three times ("lines in a play", guardar as vossas falas "made up") and asked for palavras; the blind ambiguity reader understood it at once; ditos (118:11) drew no remark from either. falas stands under D2 — the ruling of the set most exposed to the ear. Heard (118:33–80): the singular o vosso dito was refused by the stylist in all six places (118:38, 41, 50, 58, 67, 76), each time for palavra; the blind reader understood it everywhere; see the formula row secúndum elóquium tuum. 118:42 (verbum … sermónibus) decides locally for uma palavra … nas vossas palavras. Heard (118:81–128): the clause passed the stylist twice and the blind reader (118:82 à espera do que dissestes, 118:116 segundo o que dissestes); the plural os vossos ditos was refused by the stylist twice at 118:103; the noun o dito da vossa justiça (118:123, decided locally as D16 asks) refused by him twice, understood by the blind reader; the Latinist marked the clause's past tense minor on his third reading. See the row elóquium — evidence from 118:81–128 D26 (draft 17): the clause now serves the plural too — o que dissestes in all twenty places of Ps 118 (the stylist refused ditos 8 times of 8 and passed the clause at 118:148, 158, 162 when re-read); the one noun left is 118:123 o dito da vossa justiça. Outside Ps 118 the psalm decides locally (11:7, 17:31, 104:19, 147:4 have a genitive or are subjects). Ps 11:7, the first elóquium outside Ps 118 (D26 asked for a local decision): Elóquia Dómini, elóquia casta → As palavras do Senhor são palavras puras. Why palavra here: the clause cannot repeat as a predicate (the Latin's figure is the noun said twice), so it would drop a repetition (rule 2); draft 1's Os ditos do Senhor são ditos castos was refused by the stylist ("máximas moralmente recatadas") — the ninth refusal of plural ditos, now with a genitive and an adjective — and the blind reader paraphrased it palavras puras; the psalm has no verbum or sermo, so nothing collides; DRB and MS1932 have words. The Latinist passed both drafts. 17:31 elóquia Dómini igne examináta should follow (as palavras do Senhor examinadas no fogo); 147:4 (elóquium + sermo in one verse) and 106:11 must still decide locally. Options kept in Ps 11: ditos castos / puros, the clause O que o Senhor disse é puro. Ps 17:31, as D27 ruled: as palavras do Senhor, examinadas no fogo — held; the Latinist passed it; the blind reader heard it rightly. Ps 18 has all three in eleven verses: verbum 18:3 (a palavra), 18:5 (as palavras deles); sermónes 18:4 (nem palavras); elóquia 18:15a. Draft 1 kept elóquia apart as D26's clause (E há de agradar o que disser a minha boca): the Latinist marked it MAJOR (a future act of saying the Latin lacks; erunt ut compláceant flattened) and the stylist named it the psalm's worst line; draft 2 took his line, E as palavras da minha boca serão tais que agradem — outside Ps 118 elóquia is palavras (11:7, 17:31, D27), so the psalm follows them and the three Latin words meet in one Portuguese word here. Passed three gates. Merged from a duplicate row (the word alone, working beside this settled row, 2026-09-21 review): verbum — palavra · working · Nunc dimittis (233) secúndum verbum tuum → segundo a vossa palavra (the same phrase: 118:25, 118:65, 118:107 — 118:25 now translated so, word for word); see the row verbum / sermo / elóquium Ps 32:6 Verbo Dómini cæli firmáti sunt → Pela palavra do Senhor os céus foram firmados — kept palavra, not explained (the brief); the Trinity versicle (Pent01-0) and Gregory's homily in C1.txt read Verbum as the Son, and the blind reader heard the creative word first. Pelo Verbo is option 2 in Ps 32's decision verbo.
sémitathe concordance Ps 8 Ps 17 Ps 22 Ps 24 Ps 26 Ps 118veredasettled (D15)Ps 118:35 Guiai-me à vereda dos vossos mandamentos (118:105 to come) — the blind reader listed vereda as unknown, the stylist let it pass; τρίβος, 14× in the psalter, often beside via (24:4, 26:11, 76:20); alternatives senda, trilha Ps 8:9 as veredas do mar: the blind reader again listed veredas as unknown; kept (D15). Ps 17:46 a sémitis suis → fora das suas veredas: veredas listed as unknown by the blind reader once more; kept (D15). Ps 22:3 pelas veredas da justiça (the blind reader listed veredas unknown again). Ps 24:4b e ensinai-me as vossas veredas — veredas unknown to a blind reader again (Pss 22, 24).
ǽquitasPs 10 Ps 16 Ps 118equidadeworkingPs 118:40 na vossa equidade, 118:75 os vossos juízos são equidade (144, 172 to come) — the blind reader listed equidade as unknown; keeps retidão for diréctio (118:7) Ps 10:8 o seu rosto viu a equidade: listed as unknown by the blind reader twice more; kept. Ps 16:2 óculi tui vídeant æquitátes (the only plural, grep) → vejam os vossos olhos as equidades: the stylist heard 'as equidades' as technical, the plural unnatural, and asked for as coisas justas (refused: justus's word); the blind reader listed equidades as unknown (the word's fifth 'unknown'). Draft 2 tried the singular a equidade: the Latinist marked the lost plural MAJOR, so draft 3 returned to it (as 10:8 as justiças). The row's word is now the most often unknown of the working rows; retidão is diréctio's, justiça justítia's — if it is to change, the candidate that collides with nothing is o que é reto (DRB's clause). Ps 36:37 vide æquitátem → vê a equidade: unknown to the blind reader again (the antiphon Custódi innocéntiam * et vide æquitátem); kept.

mirificárePs 4 Ps 15 Ps 16 Ps 30fazer maravilhosoworking4:4, 15:3, 16:7, 30:22; must not collide with magnificáre → engrandecer Ps 15:3 mirificávit omnes voluntátes meas in eis → fez maravilhosas todas as minhas vontades neles (no subject supplied; the blind reader heard the Lord). The stylist wanted tornou maravilhosas — refused for this row (4:4), kept as an option. Ps 16:7 Mirífica misericórdias tuas (named in this row) → Fazei maravilhosas as vossas misericórdias (the imperative; the plural kept). The stylist asked for Tornai maravilhosas — the second stylist in two psalms (15:3, 16:7) to hear fazer maravilhoso as translated; refused for the row, his verb is option 3 in Ps 16. The Latinist passed it three times. For whoever rules the row: tornar maravilhoso keeps the -ficáre as well as fazer does. Ps 30:22 mirificávit misericórdiam suam mihi → fez maravilhosa a sua misericórdia para mim: the stylist again called the build a calque (mostrou de modo maravilhoso, refused, option); the blind reader did not stumble; passed the Latinist twice.
magnificárePs 9 Ps 17 Ps 40engrandecerworkingPs 11:5 Engrandeceremos a nossa língua (the boast in the Latin's image; MS1932 explains it) — echoed by 11:4 magníloquam → que fala grandezas. Ps 17:51 Magníficans salútes Regis ejus → Engrandecendo as salvações do seu Rei (the plural kept; the participle as a gerund, dangling as the Latin's does). Ps 19:6 magnificábimur → seremos engrandecidos (the Latin; the LXX has μεγαλυνθησόμεθα). Ps 40:10 magnificávit super me supplantatiónem → fez grande contra mim a rasteira: a thing made great, not praise or boasting; draft 1's engrandeceu … a sua rasteira was the stylist's worst line (no such collocation) and engrandeceu was unknown to the blind reader there. Local; engrandecer stays the row's word.
sanctus (ὅσιος)Ps 4 Ps 5 Ps 11 Ps 14 Ps 15 Ps 19 Ps 21 Ps 23 Ps 27 Ps 28 Ps 29 Ps 31 Ps 32 Ps 33 Ps 42 Ps 133santoworkingPs 4 Ps 31:6 orábit ad te omnis sanctus → todo santo orará a vós (the stylist's order). The blind reader heard the canonized saints first, as at 30:24; kept; cada santo, todo homem santo (MS1932) options.
compúngiPs 4 Ps 29 Ps 34compungir-seopenPs 4; stylist prefers arrepender-se Ps 29:13 et non compúngar → e eu não seja compungido (draft 2): draft 1's reflexive não me compunja (4:5's form) drew the Latinist's minor (the voice), the stylist's worst line («exige decifração») and the blind reader's unknown word; the passive participle keeps the Latin's voice and is current (coração compungido). The stylist's não seja ferido (the image, plainer) is the option. So the row now has the reflexive for the imperative (4:5 compungi-vos) and the passive for the passive. Ps 34:16 nec compúncti → e não compungidos (the passive participle, 29:13; the second foram dropped with the Latin's ellipsis, after the stylist). The blind reader listed compungidos unknown; kept. The stylist's não sentiram remorso is option 4.
in idípsumPs 4 Ps 33 Ps 40a um só tempoopen4:9, 33:4, 40:7, 61:10, 73:6, 121:3 — may not bear one rendering everywhere Ps 33:4 et exaltémus nomen ejus in idípsum → e exaltemos juntos o seu nome (ἐπὶ τὸ αὐτό, the Greek of in unum; Hebrew יחדו): persons acting together, so juntos, as in unum; a um só tempo would say simultaneity. Ruled locally under deferred D9; passed the Latinist twice, heard rightly. Proposal for the row: persons acting together → juntos; one agent or a thing → a um só tempo (4:9). Ps 40:7b et loquebátur in idípsum (one man speaking; the Greek puts ἐπὶ τὸ αὐτό with the next verse's whispering) → e falava no mesmo sentido (DRB 'to the same purpose'): neither juntos (a plural) nor a um só tempo fits one speaker. Ruled locally; the blind reader heard both readings the Latin allows (the same talk; in agreement with the others); the stylist's do mesmo modo (manner) is an option; the Latinist passed it twice. Third local rendering: evidence that D9 cannot be one word.

speráre inPs 21 Ps 25 Ps 26 Ps 30 Ps 31 Ps 33 Ps 35 Ps 36 Ps 90 Ps 117esperar emsettled (D36)Ps 90:2, 90:4, 90:14 — the blind ambiguity reader heard it first as waiting for God, second as trusting (ps090/critic/v1.ambiguity.json); kept, but whoever rules on it should know Ps 7:2 em vós esperei: the blind reader heard hope first, waiting second. Ps 20:8 rex sperat in Dómino → o rei espera no Senhor (heard as trust first — the stylist's best line). Ps 24:20 porque esperei em vós, apart from sustinére → esperar por (24:3, 24:5, 24:21). Ps 35:8 in tégmine alárum tuárum sperábunt → esperarão ao abrigo das vossas asas — the blind reader heard waiting first again.
confitéri (to God)Ps 6 Ps 7 Ps 9 Ps 17 Ps 27 Ps 29 Ps 31 Ps 32 Ps 53 Ps 117dar graças a (darei graças ao vosso nome) — proposed by Ps 53:8 in place of the pencilled louvarsettled (D5); the noun conféssio stays openheavy — confessar / dar graças / louvar / celebrar; still wants a word study; confessar for sins (31:5). Ps 53's grounds: confitéri stands in one verse with laudáre (34:18, 108:30, 105:47), so louvar would make one word of two; grátias ágere never occurs in the psalms, so dar graças is free; confitéri to God always has a dative and dar graças a keeps it; Dai graças is safe at the vós imperative (dai ≠ dei). Costs: the root of confessing is faint; the noun conféssio will not always follow (ação de graças fits 41:5, 94:2, 99:3b, 146:7, not 95:6, 103:1, 110:3, 148:14a). Ps 117 follows this until Gustavo rules Ps 6:6, the test D5 named: no inferno, porém, quem vos dará graças? — holds: the dative is kept (vos), it is as natural as MS1932's and DM1962's louvará, and it closes on a paroxytone; the Latinist passed it twice, neither the stylist nor the blind reader remarked on it. Ps 7:18, a place the coordinator named: Darei graças ao Senhor segundo a sua justiça — holds (MS1932 Glorificarei, DRB give glory); passed the Latinist twice, no remark from the stylist or the blind reader. Ps 17:50 Proptérea confitébor tibi in natiónibus, Dómine → Por isso vos darei graças entre as nações, Senhor — D5 held; no reader remarked. Ps 29 has it three times: 29:5 confitémini memóriæ sanctitátis ejus → dai graças à memória da sua santidade (the blind reader found the dative to a 'memory' a little odd, and understood it), 29:10b Numquid confitébitur tibi pulvis → Acaso o pó vos dará graças (6:6's question again), 29:13 in ætérnum confitébor tibi → para sempre vos darei graças. D5 held; no reader objected. Ps 31:5b — confitéri of sin → confessar (D5): Confitébor advérsum me injustítiam meam Dómino → Contra mim mesmo, confessarei ao Senhor a minha injustiça (draft 2, the stylist's order: the blind reader heard draft 1's confessarei contra mim a minha injustiça as an injustice done to me). Ps 34:18 Confitébor tibi in ecclésia magna, * in pópulo gravi laudábo te → Eu vos darei graças na grande assembleia, * num povo numeroso vos louvarei — D5's named verse. gravis → numeroso (the Latinist, major on draft 1's grave; the blind reader heard 'gravely ill'). Ps 41:5b the noun, exsultatiónis, et confessiónis → de exultação e de ação de graças (the family beside the refrain's darei graças); the stylist's louvor (laus's) option 2.
salutáre / salusPs 3 Ps 11 Ps 13 Ps 19 Ps 20 Ps 23 Ps 26 Ps 34 Ps 39 Ps 41 Ps 42 Ps 118salvaçãosettled (D6)whether the two must differ; Ps 90:16 salutáre meum → a minha salvação; Nunc dimittis (233) salutáre tuum → a vossa salvação, matched — the blind reader heard it as "someone or something that brings the Lord's salvation", not as God being saved Ps 11:6b Ponam in salutári → Porei na salvação (no object, as the Latin; the blind reader supplied "the poor" without stumbling, twice).
ánima meaPs 6 Ps 7 Ps 10 Ps 12 Ps 15 Ps 16 Ps 18 Ps 21 Ps 22 Ps 23 Ps 24 Ps 25 Ps 26 Ps 29 Ps 30 Ps 32 Ps 33 Ps 34 Ps 40 Ps 41 Ps 42 Ps 53 Ps 118a minha almaworkingPs 53:5–6 shows the cost: in quæsiérunt ánimam meam (the idiom for seeking someone's death) the blind reader heard the spiritual soul first and "my life" second; MS1932, pt-PT and DM1962 write vida there; kept, with vida as an option

in ætérnumPs 5 Ps 9 Ps 14 Ps 23 Ps 27 Ps 28 Ps 29 Ps 30 Ps 40 Ps 118para sempresettled (D23)D23: one with in sǽculum — one Greek phrase (εἰς τὸν αἰῶνα), merged by Douay-Rheims and Matos Soares 1932; the refrain of Ps 135 will be that of Ps 117. Eternamente is the option in Ps 118. Before D23: kept distinct from in sǽculum on purpose. Ps 118:89, 93, 98, 111, 112 (one slot, decision in_aeternum): the Latinist and the blind reader passed it, Eternamente não esquecerei (118:93, = never) included; the stylist never named the word, but four of his proposed lines put para sempre in its place (118:98, 111 twice, 112), and its position in 118:111 had to move twice. Evidence that the ear leans to para sempre; merging would cost the distinction from in sǽculum (118:44 has that) — for the main session, with Ps 135 in view D23 held at 118:142, 144, 152, 160 (slot {aet} of decision in_aeternum): A vossa justiça é justiça para sempre — the stylist's best line of the portion on both readings; são equidade para sempre; que os fundastes para sempre; todos os juízos da vossa justiça são para sempre (natural order, from the stylist: para sempre são todos … hung until its subject came). No reader of four Latinist, two stylist and two blind readings remarked on the phrase. Ps 11:8 desta geração para sempre — D23 applied. Ps 14:5b — D23 under a negation: para sempre não será abalado (the 118:93 build). The Latinist passed it twice and the blind reader heard 'never'; the stylist refused it as foreign and asked for jamais — for Gustavo's ear: this is where D23 costs most. 5:12a exultarão para sempre (Ps 5 draft 4; drafts 1–3 had eternamente, made before D23 and never swept — review 2026-09-21).
in sǽculum sǽculiPs 9 Ps 17 Ps 20 Ps 21 Ps 40 Ps 117 Ps 118pelos séculos dos séculossettled (D27)Ps 9:6 = 9:37 in ætérnum, et in sǽculum sǽculi → para sempre, e pelos séculos dos séculos (one slot in Ps 9's decision saeculi): the Latinist asked for the singular pelo século do século five times (9:6 ×3, 9:37 ×2; major once, as part of a major); the stylist asked twice to reverse the order for the cadence. Held both; wants the row settled. D27: settled — the plural is how the phrase lives in Portuguese; number is grammar (D2). Ps 20:5 in sǽculum, et in sǽculum sǽculi → para sempre, e pelos séculos dos séculos; 20:7 in sǽculum sǽculi at the head of the verse (Porque pelos séculos dos séculos fareis dele uma bênção). The Latinist's minors on the plural came on draft 1 and again at the gate — refused (D27). Ps 18:10 pérmanens in sǽculum sǽculi → permanece pelos séculos dos séculos: the Latinist asked four times (minor) for pelo século do século, the stylist once for a cadence change — both refused (D27). Ps 21:27 vivent corda eórum in sǽculum sǽculi → viverão os seus corações pelos séculos dos séculos: the Latinist's minor twice (singular), refused (D27); the stylist asked for the formula first (proparoxytone final) — refused, the Latin's own cadence; option in decision saeculi. Ps 36:27, 36:29 → pelos séculos dos séculos: the Latinist asked the singular (minor) at both, twice (drafts 3 and 4); the stylist asked to move the formula off the final (proparoxytone). Both refused under D27.
beátus virPs 1 Ps 32bem-aventurado o homemsettled (D19); was: open — Ps 1:1 rules for it and asks for a settlingPs 1 (decisions beatus, vir). beátus 29× (grep), nearly always first in its verse; the Latin psalter never has felix. Bem-aventurado because feliz is first a mood today and beátus is a verdict on a life (L&S quotes Cicero's definition), because it is the one word heard as a macarism, and because it serves every construction (143:15 beátum dixérunt, 40:3 beátum fáciat); MS1932 has it in 1:1 and 2:13, Ps 118:1–2 already uses it; neither blind reader remarked on it. Cost: 5 syllables for 3, at the head of the verse where it is cheapest. Live alternative: feliz (DM1962; the plainer word, which is what D2 favours — the ruling most worth Gustavo's eye); ditoso refused as literary. vir → homem: merges with homo (Beátus homo 83:13, 93:12); varão is the only way to keep them apart and is archaic Ps 31:2 Beátus vir, cui non imputávit Dóminus peccátum → Bem-aventurado o homem a quem o Senhor não atribuiu pecado; 31:1 plural Beáti quorum → Bem-aventurados aqueles cujas (the colon +8, D19's cost; = Rom 4:7).

adjutóriumPs 7 Ps 34 Ps 37 Ps 90auxílioworkingPs 90; Deus, in adjutórium meum inténde will want the same word Ps 7:11 Justo é o meu auxílio vindo do Senhor — the Latin's reading, not the Hebrew's shield; the blind reader heard "the help is just" before "the helper is just".
suscéptorPs 3 Ps 17 Ps 53 Ps 90amparosettled (D19)Ps 90:2; recurs 3:4, 17:3c, 41:9, 45:8, 53:6, 58:10, 61:3, 88:27, 118:114, 143:2; must stay apart from protéctor → protetor; MS1932 defensor; Ps 53:6 tries it with a genitive (o amparo da minha alma) and it holds — alternatives there arrimo (DM1962), defensor Ps 17:3c e o meu amparo: held against the Latinist — minor on draft 1 (amparador), MAJOR on draft 2 with the words unchanged and another fix (aquele que me ampara). The pair noun/verb is heard across the psalm: 17:36a déxtera tua suscépit me → a vossa direita me amparou (one decision susceptor, two slots). Merged from a duplicate row (the pair, beside the rows it combined, 2026-09-21 review): suscéptor + suscípere together — amparo … amparar · settled (D19); was: open — Ps 3 finds the pair holds; ready to settle · Ps 3:4 sois o meu amparo … 3:6 porque o Senhor me amparou (= 117:13b word for word): the one psalm with noun and verb two verses apart (Greek ἀντιλήμπτωρ … ἀντιλήμψεται), and the echo is heard; the abstract noun sits well beside a minha glória; no reader remarked on it. See the rows suscéptor and suscípere Ps 29:2 quóniam suscepísti me → porque me amparastes (the Matins antiphon and the versicle of 12-08 and of Our Lady Aparecida sing this colon, grep); passed the Latinist twice, heard rightly.

liberárePs 30 Ps 33 Ps 36 Ps 90livrarworkingPs 90:3, 90:14
eríperePs 6 Ps 16 Ps 33 Ps 118libertar where no source is named (6:5 libertai a minha alma, 21:9, 33:8, 90:15, 118:153, 118:170); arrancar where a de … follows or the source is named (16:13, 17:18, 30:16, 31:7, 34:10b …)openPs 90:15; kept apart from liberáre (MS1932 and DRB merge them); works at the vós imperative (Arrancai-me); alternative libertar; Ps 53:9 ex omni tribulatióne eripuísti me → de toda tribulação me arrancastes Ps 6:5 finds a place where it fails: éripe ánimam meam with no source named — the only such éripe of the 17 in the psalter (grep) — would be arrancai a minha alma, "tear out my soul". Ps 6 has libertai a minha alma (this row's own alternative; DM1962's verb; safe: past libertei). Wherever a de … follows, or the object is me, arrancar is untouched. If one verb is wanted everywhere, libertar is the one that never fails; it stays apart from liberáre → livrar only by a suffix. Ps 118:153, 170 — éripe me with NO source named (Vide humilitátem meam, et éripe me; secúndum elóquium tuum éripe me): the row said that with me as object arrancar is untouched, but e arrancai-me with nothing to be torn from leaves the sentence hanging, as arrancai a minha alma did in Ps 6. Ps 118 has libertai-me in both (the Diurnal's verb at 153; safe); passed all readers. Evidence for the row's own last sentence: libertar is the verb that never fails. Ps 17:18, 30, 44, 49 — four times, each with a source named (de inimícis, a tentatióne, de contradictiónibus, a viro iníquo) → arrancar throughout; no reader remarked, and serei arrancado da tentação (17:30) was heard rightly. Ps 16:13 éripe ánimam meam ab ímpio → arrancai a minha alma do ímpio — a source named, so arrancar as the row says; no reader stumbled (the stylist wanted tirai, refused). Ps 21:9 erípiat eum (no source named; the mockers' words) → que ele o liberte — the row's own conclusion (libertar never fails), with livrar left to 21:5 liberásti. Mt 27:43 (Vulgate, fetched) has liberet nunc, si vult eum — another wording. See the new row éruere. Ps 30:16 Éripe me de manu inimicórum meórum → Arrancai-me da mão dos meus inimigos (a source named); no reader remark. Ps 33:5 ex ómnibus tribulatiónibus meis erípuit me → e me arrancou de todas as minhas tribulações (draft 2 the verb first, the stylist's rhyme); 33:8 erípiet eos → e os libertará. Passed the Latinist twice. 90:15 erípiam eum → eu o libertarei (Ps 90 draft 3, review 2026-09-21): the one place left with arrancar and no source; its decision had argued that na tribulação supplies one, before the evidence above existed.

verúmtamenPs 38 Ps 90todaviaopenPs 90:8; 31:6, 38:6–7, 38:12, 48:16, 61:5–6, 61:10, 67:22, 72:18, 84:10, 139:14 Ps 31:6b Verúmtamen in dilúvio aquárum multárum → Todavia, no dilúvio das muitas águas — applied. appropinquárePs 37aproximar-seworkingPs 90:7, 90:10 Ps 31:6b ad eum non approximábunt → não se aproximarão dele; 31:9b qui non appróximant ad te → que não se aproximam de vós — approximáre (the only two places, grep) takes the same verb.
tabernáculumPs 14 Ps 17 Ps 18 Ps 26 Ps 42 Ps 90tendaopenPs 90:10; the same word must serve God's tent (14:1, 30:21, 60:5) Ps 14:1 Dómine, quis habitábit in tabernáculo tuo? → Senhor, quem habitará na vossa tenda? — God's tent, as this row foresaw; passed the Latinist twice; the blind reader heard 'living with the Lord' first and the concrete tent second. no vosso tabernáculo (MS1932) is the option. 31 lines (grep), God's and men's alike (77:51 in tabernáculis Cham, 83:11 tabernáculis peccatórum), which tabernáculo (a church's, in Brazil) could not serve. Ps 18:6a In sole pósuit tabernáculum suum → No sol pôs a sua tenda — the Latin's reading (the tent in the sun; the Hebrew's is for the sun); passed every reader; tabernáculo (MS1932) the option. Ps 42:3 et in tabernácula tua → e às vossas tendas (plural kept; the blind reader heard market stalls — noted, the row stands). Ps 41:5 in locum tabernáculi admirábilis → ao lugar da tenda admirável; the blind reader heard tent first (casa de Deus beside it).
flagéllumPs 31 Ps 34 Ps 90flagelosettled (D39)Ps 90:10 Ps 31:10 Multa flagélla peccatóris → Os flagelos do pecador são muitos (the copula supplied; subject first so the colon does not rhyme with Senhor at the final). The blind reader listed flagelos as unknown and heard 'the punishments the sinner receives'; kept. Ps 34:15 congregáta sunt super me flagélla → juntaram-se sobre mim os flagelos; the blind reader listed flagelos unknown again (heard calamities); kept. Ps 38:10 plagas tuas → os vossos flagelos: plaga here is μάστιγας, the Greek of flagélla (31:10, 34:15), so D15 gives one word (MS1932, DRB 'scourges'). flagelos unknown to the blind reader again (heard punishments / plagues, which is the sense); golpes is option 2. plaga elsewhere (63:8 wounds, 72:4) decides locally.
mandárePs 7 Ps 32 Ps 41 Ps 90 Ps 118dar ordemopenPs 90:11; loses the link with mandáta → mandamentos; MS1932 mandar. Ps 118:4 Tu mandásti mandáta tua is translated with mandar (Vós mandastes que os vossos mandamentos…) because there the link is the line; 118:138 will want the same — so either mandar everywhere or dar ordem except beside mandáta Ps 7:7b in præcépto quod mandásti: a noun object, which dar ordem cannot take → no decreto que mandastes (the row's first alternative, mandar, of one family with mandamentos). The stylist heard mandar um decreto as sending it and asked for ordenastes (option there, and the Ps 2 agent's trial wording); the blind reader heard "o decreto que emitistes". Three candidates now — dar ordem, mandar, ordenar — wants a ruling; 12 verses (grep: 7:7b, 32:9, 41:9, 77:5, 77:23, 90:11, 104:8, 110:9a, 118:4, 118:138, 132:3b, 148:5). Ps 118:138 Mandásti justítiam testimónia tua: with a bare object Mandastes os vossos testemunhos was heard by the blind reader as "you SENT reports" — mandar is two verbs in Brazil. Mandar que + subjunctive (as 118:4) is safe but the Latinist marked the added clause major; the verse now reads Ordenastes como justiça os vossos testemunhos (heard as "Determinastes"). So: mandar only with que or beside mandáta; with a thing as object, ordenar. Ps 32:9 ipse mandávit, et creáta sunt → ele ordenou, e foram criadas — no object, and mandou alone is heard 'sent' or colloquial; ordenar as 118:138. 148:5 is the near twin (formula row). Passed the Latinist twice. Ps 41:9 mandávit Dóminus misericórdiam suam → o Senhor mandou — with a thing as object dar ordem cannot stand (as 7:7b); mandar holds 'command' and 'send'.
custodírePs 11 Ps 16 Ps 38 Ps 118guardarworkingPs 90:11 Ps 16:4 ego custodívi vias duras → eu guardei caminhos duros (as 17:22 guardei os caminhos do Senhor): the blind reader was unsure what guardar caminhos means (followed? watched? remembered?), heard 'followed' first. 16:8a custódi me → Guardai-me (15:1 had left the verb free for it). Ps 18:12 custódit ea, in custodiéndis illis → os guarda, em guardá-los; the blind reader heard 'obeys' first. Ps 38:2 Custódiam vias meas → Guardarei os meus caminhos; 38:2b the noun Pósui ori meo custódiam → Pus guarda à minha boca — the Latin's (and Greek's) echo kept by verb and noun of one root. The blind reader heard 'be careful how I live' first. 140:3 Pone, Dómine, custódiam ori meo should copy the noun.

sancta / sanctum (a place)Ps 4 Ps 5 Ps 11 Ps 14 Ps 15 Ps 19 Ps 21 Ps 23 Ps 27 Ps 28 Ps 29 Ps 31 Ps 32 Ps 33 Ps 42 Ps 133o lugar santoopenPs 133:2 in sancta; kept apart from sanctuárium → santuário (72:17, 73:7, 82:13, 88:40); the Latinist wants the plural (os lugares santos), refused because in Brazil that names the Holy Land sites; in sancto (21:4, 59:8, 62:3, 76:14) is not always a place and is not settled by this Ps 19:3 de sancto → do lugar santo (the blind reader heard an earthly sanctuary first, heaven second — the Latin's two senses; 19:7b has de cælo sancto suo for heaven); the Preces feriales adapt the verse (formula row). Deus (vocative)Deus, bareopenPs 53:3–4; the Senhor rule extended (no ó unless the Latin has o); MS1932, pt-PT and DM1962 all write ó Deus; will serve Deus, in adjutórium meum inténde Ps 5:11a julgai-os, Deus follows it; the colon is four syllables for the Latin's seven, the first place where ó Deus would help the chant (option there). 5:3 meu Rei e meu Deus.
salvum fácerePs 17 Ps 19 Ps 21 Ps 24 Ps 27 Ps 30 Ps 32 Ps 33 Ps 35 Ps 53 Ps 118salvar (salvai-me)workingPs 53:3; the formula Salvum me fac (11:2, 68:2) and salvum fac (19:10); merges with salváre, as the Greek does (σῴζω); safe at the vós imperative (salvai ≠ salvei); calque fazei-me salvo refused Ps 11:2 Salvai-me, Senhor — the formula Salvum me fac, Dómine (also 117:25 O Dómine, salvum me fac).
judicáre, júdica mePs 5 Ps 9 Ps 34 Ps 53 Ps 118julgar (julgai-me), bareopenPs 53:3; 7:9b, 25:1, 34:24, 42:1 (Júdica me, Deus, et discérne causam meam — causa is a word of its own there); the blind reader heard "pass sentence on me" before "do justice to my cause" — the Latin is open in the same way; MS1932 julga a minha causa Ps 5:11a júdica illos → julgai-os: heard by the blind reader first as "condemn them" — the Latin's range. Ps 7:9b Julgai-me, Senhor, segundo a minha justiça (= 25:1's opening), 7:8b o Senhor julga os povos — here the sense is plainly "do me justice"; no reader heard condemnation. Ps 118:154 Júdica judícium meum: Julgai o meu juízo was heard by the blind reader FIRST as "evaluate the opinion I formed" (o meu julgamento runs the same risk). Now Julgai em juízo a minha causa (from the stylist): the law-word and the figure kept, the right sense heard; passed the Latinist twice. Cost: causa is another Latin word — 9:5 judícium meum et causam meam and 34:23 set the two side by side and must decide locally.
virtus (δύναμις), in virtúte tuaPs 17 Ps 20 Ps 21 Ps 23 Ps 28 Ps 29 Ps 30 Ps 32 Ps 37 Ps 53 Ps 117poderopenPs 53:3, for the singular in virtúte tua only; heavy (72 verses; Dóminus virtútum, 117:16 fecit virtútem) — wants a word study; must stay apart from fortitúdo and poténtia (64:7, 67:36); virtude is a moral quality today; DRB strength Ps 17:33, 17:40 præcínxit me virtúte … præcinxísti me virtúte ad bellum → me cingiu de poder … me cingistes de poder para a guerra — the row's word, kept apart from 17:2 fortitúdo → força in the same psalm (DRB and MS1932 have strength / força here, which would merge them). Passed the Latinist twice; no other reader remarked. Ps 20:2 = 20:14a in virtúte tua → no vosso poder; 20:14b the plural virtútes tuas → os vossos poderes (the Latinist's MAJOR on the singular ao vosso poder: plural, and the object sung, not the addressee). Ps 21:16 Áruit tamquam testa virtus mea (ἰσχύς) → O meu vigor secou-se como um caco: a man's bodily strength drying up — poder would be authority, força is fortitúdo's. MS1932's vigor. Proposal: virtus of a man's own strength → vigor (30:11 infirmáta est … virtus mea, 37:11 dereliquit me virtus mea, 70:9 dum defécerit virtus mea — grep before use); poder stays for God's (in virtúte tua) and for 17:33, 40. Passed the Latinist twice; no reader remarked. Ps 23:10 Dóminus virtútum → o Senhor dos poderes (its first place of 16: 45:8, 45:12, 47:9, 58:6, 67:13 Rex virtútum, 68:7, 79:5, 8, 15, 20, 83:2, 4, 9, 13, 88:9 — grep); the plural as 20:14b. Passed the Latinist twice; the blind reader heard 'the Lord who has all powers' (or 'of powerful beings' — the Greek's δυνάμεις, the hosts, are both). Options dos exércitos (DRB, the Hebrew's Sabaoth), das virtudes (MS1932). Formula row proposed; wants a ruling before Ps 45. Ps 28:4 Vox Dómini in virtúte → A voz do Senhor com poder; 28:11 Dóminus virtútem pópulo suo dabit → O Senhor dará poder ao seu povo (God's virtus given; one word for the psalm's two places). Passed the Latinist twice; no reader remarked; the stylist named 28:11 the best line. força (DRB) the option. Ps 29:8 præstitísti decóri meo virtútem → destes vigor ao meu esplendor — the proposal vigor for a man's strength applied (given by God to the man's decor, not God's own power); passed the Latinist twice; poder the option. Ps 37:11 derelíquit me virtus mea → abandonou-me o meu vigor — the proposal vigor applied at a place it named; passed the Latinist twice, no reader remarked.
aliénusPs 17 Ps 18 Ps 53estranhoworkingPs 53:5; wider than estrangeiro (anyone not one's own) Ps 17:46 Fílii aliéni … fílii aliéni → Os filhos estranhos … os filhos estranhos: the blind reader heard 'other people's children' or 'odd children' first and did not perceive foreigners — inside the row's 'not one's own'; kept, filhos alheios and filhos de estrangeiros are options. Ps 18:13 et ab aliénis parce servo tuo → e dos alheios poupai o vosso servo — three drafts: das estranhas (draft 1, the faults of others with faltas understood) failed three readers at once (Latinist MAJOR, the stylist, the blind reader, all hearing 'odd faults'); das alheias (draft 2) failed the gate MAJOR because 18:14b's masculine domináti makes its subject persons (delícta would give domináta); dos estranhos (draft 3, persons) failed the gate MAJOR for closing out others' faults. dos alheios (the Latinist's fix twice) holds both, as the masculine/neuter aliénis does, and passed. So: estranho for persons named as such (17:46 filhos estranhos); a bare substantival aliéni may want os alheios.

insúrgere in / advérsumPs 2 Ps 3 Ps 14 Ps 17 Ps 22 Ps 26 Ps 31 Ps 34 Ps 38 Ps 40 Ps 53 Ps 118levantar-se contraopenPs 53:5; 3:2, 17:40, 17:49, 26:12, 43:6, 58:2, 91:12, 108:28; 85:14 is a near-twin of 53:5 (insurrexérunt super me … quæsiérunt ánimam meam: et non proposuérunt te in conspéctu suo) — whoever does Ps 85 should echo it; shares levantar-se with God's exsúrgere, told apart by contra; alternative insurgir-se Ps 17:40, 17:49 (both in this row's list): os que contra mim se levantavam (the stylist's order), acima dos que se levantam contra mim; passed all readers.
quǽrerePs 9 Ps 33 Ps 36buscarworkingPs 4:3, Ps 53:5 quæsiérunt ánimam meam → buscaram a minha alma Ps 23:6 quæréntium … quæréntium → dos que o buscam … dos que buscam (the repetition kept). requírere (24:10) takes procurar, apart.
conspéctus, ante conspéctum suumPs 5 Ps 9 Ps 14 Ps 15 Ps 17 Ps 21 Ps 22 Ps 35 Ps 37 Ps 40 Ps 53 Ps 118vista, diante da sua vistaopenPs 53:5; kept apart from óculus → olho, which comes in 53:9; in conspéctu is everywhere and may want diante de; MS1932 diante de si, DRB before their eyes Ps 5:9 proposes in conspéctu + genitive → à vista de (endireitai o meu caminho à vossa vista): keeps this row's noun and the seeing; 51 verses have in conspéctu (grep). Tried on 22:5 Preparastes à minha vista uma mesa, 137:1b À vista dos anjos, 115:15 Preciosa à vista do Senhor — it fails none. In Ps 5 it stays apart from astábo tibi → diante de vós and ante óculos tuos → diante dos vossos olhos. The blind reader heard "make my conduct right in your eyes" first. Options na vossa presença, diante de vós. Ps 118:168–170 — in conspéctu tuo three verses running, two of them with a verb of MOTION (Appropínquet … in conspéctu tuo, Intret … in conspéctu tuo): à vossa vista is excellent in 118:168 (todos os meus caminhos estão à vossa vista) and impossible in 118:170 (Entre o meu pedido à vossa vista). Ps 118 has the noun phrase a vossa presença in all three, each verb taking its own preposition (estão na … · Aproxime-se … da … · Entre … na … — the stylist refused Aproxime-se na); passed thereafter. For whoever studies the 51 verses: à vista de fails with verbs of motion. Ps 9:20 in conspéctu tuo → à vossa vista, 9:26a Non est Deus in conspéctu ejus → Não há Deus à vista dele: the noun kept; the blind reader heard 9:26a as 'God does not count for him' — the sense. Evidence for vista over diante de. Ps 14:4a in conspéctu ejus → À vista dele: the stylist asked for Aos olhos dele ('à vista' sounded like physical presence) — refused, olho is óculus's; the blind reader heard 'aos olhos do homem descrito', the judging look, rightly. Evidence for vista again (9:20, 9:26a, 14:4a). Ps 17 has in conspéctu four times in 17:2–25 (one decision conspectu): à vista de where it is a predicate (17:23 todos os seus juízos estão à minha vista; 17:25 à vista dos seus olhos), na sua presença where it hangs beside a noun (17:7b o meu clamor, na sua presença, entrou nos seus ouvidos; 17:13 Diante do clarão, na sua presença, passaram as nuvens). In 17:13 the genitive o clarão da sua presença was refused by the Latinist (major): the phrase is adverbial, not the brightness's owner. Evidence that à vista serves predicates, presença the rest. Ps 15:8 Providébam Dóminum in conspéctu meo semper → Eu via o Senhor sempre à minha vista (the Latinist's line); the stylist's diante de mim refused for this row's noun, kept as an option. Acts 2:25 quotes it (fetched). Ps 16:15 apparébo conspéctui tuo (a dative, no in) → aparecerei à vossa vista — the row's noun as a predicate place; the blind reader heard 'appear before God as a just man'. Ps 21 (one decision conspectu, three slots): 21:26 vota mea reddam in conspéctu timéntium eum → à vista dos que o temem (the place of witnesses); 21:28b adorábunt in conspéctu ejus and 21:30 in conspéctu ejus cadent → na sua presença (with an act of worship; adorarão à vista dele is stiff) — the Ps 17 split again. No reader remarked. Ps 22:5 in conspéctu meo → à minha vista (the row's noun, as 5:9's à vista de). Ps 35:3 in conspéctu ejus → à sua vista (whose — God's or his own — left open, as the Latin; the blind reader heard God's).
adjuvárePs 39 Ps 53 Ps 118auxiliar (Deus me auxilia)openPs 53:6; keeps one family with adjutórium → auxílio and adjútor (117:7 next); plainer alternative ajudar splits the family. Ps 118:86, 117 auxiliai-me (safe at the imperative); no reader remarked Ps 21:12 non est qui ádjuvet → não há quem auxilie (no object, as the Latin); no reader remarked. Ps 39:14b Dómine, ad adjuvándum me réspice → Senhor, olhai para auxiliar-me (draft 1 para me auxiliar was heard 'look at me' by the stylist; the enclitic after the infinitive mends it). This is the half that Ps 69:2b shares (Dómine, ad adjuvándum me festína, the response of every Hour's opening versicle): it fixes auxiliar-me there, not inténde in adjutórium (D40, deferred). The stylist's olhai em meu auxílio refused: adjutórium's noun, and it would pre-empt D40.
avértere (+ dative)Ps 13 Ps 53desviar … paraopenPs 53:7 Avérte mala inimícis meis → Desviai os males para os meus inimigos; safe at the vós imperative (desviai ≠ desviei); can serve avérte fáciem tuam; MS1932 Faze recair … sobre Ps 12:1 avértis fáciem tuam a me → desviais de mim a vossa face (the formula; 9 lines with fáciem tuam a: 26:9, 29:8, 50:11, 68:18, 87:15, 101:3, 142:7b …; 9:32 desviou a sua face in Ps 9 agrees). Desviar keeps avértere one verb with or without a dative; the Latinist passed it twice. Ps 13:7 cum avérterit Dóminus captivitátem plebis suæ → quando o Senhor afastar o cativeiro do seu povo: draft 1 had this row's desviar; the stylist found desviar o cativeiro not Portuguese and the blind reader could not tell whether the captivity was present or threatened. Afastar (the stylist's) taken there — it shares a verb with amovére / repéllere (row amovére), which never meet captivitátem. Desviar stays right with a face (12:1) or anger (77:38, 105:24 ut avérteret iram). 84:2 avertísti captivitátem Jacob should follow Ps 13 (afastastes o cativeiro de Jacó). The twin 52:7 has convérterit (row convértere) and should differ. Ps 21:25b Nec avértit fáciem suam a me → Nem desviou de mim a sua face (the face formula, 12:1).
dispérderePs 53exterminarworkingPs 53:7; about fifteen verses, twin line 142:11b; not dispérgere (scatter, 58:12); destruir fails rule 3 (destruí-os = I destroyed them); MS1932, pt-PT Ps 11:4 Extermine o Senhor todos os lábios enganadores — the jussive with the subject after it; the blind reader heard "destroy the people who deceive". Ps 17:41 et odiéntes me disperdidísti → e exterminastes os que me odeiam. Ps 36:9 exterminabúntur (exterminári, ἐξολεθρευθήσονται) → serão exterminados — the cognate, shared with dispérdere: the two never meet in a verse (exterminári only 36:9, 79:14, grep). Ps 36 keeps the Latin's other verbs of ending apart: períre → perecer, disperíre → perecer de todo (36:22, 38; 82:11 to follow), interíre → extinguir-se (36:38; 48:18 of a man will want morrer).

latitúdo, in latitúdinePs 17 Ps 117amplidão (me escutou na amplidão)openPs 117:5 (from the stylist); a noun for the noun, nothing supplied; serves 17:20 edúxit me in latitúdinem, 118:45 ambulábam in latitúdine; largueza was heard by both blind readers as generosity; keeps lugar espaçoso free for 30:9 in loco spatióso; compare 4:2a dilatásti mihi → me abristes espaço Ps 17:20 Et edúxit me in latitúdinem → E me fez sair para a amplidão — the verse this row named; the stylist's best line of stage one; the blind reader heard a wide place and freedom. 17:37 Dilatásti gressus meos → Alargastes (same image, another word). invocárePs 13 Ps 17 Ps 19 Ps 41invocarworkingPs 117:5 Ps 19:8 invocaremos em nome do Senhor; 19:10 no dia em que vos invocarmos. Ps 41:8 Abýssus abýssum ínvocat → O abismo invoca o abismo.
adjútorPs 7 Ps 9 Ps 17 Ps 18 Ps 26 Ps 27 Ps 29 Ps 32 Ps 34 Ps 37 Ps 39 Ps 90 Ps 117 Ps 118auxílio (O Senhor é o meu auxílio)open — for Gustavo (HANDOFF). The text is uniform, auxílio 14/14; D19 names adjútor → auxílio only in passing, as what amparo stays apart from — it is not a ruling, so the row stays openPs 117:6–7; one family with adjutórium and adjuváre; the Latinist asked twice (minor) for the agent noun auxiliador — refused for length and currency, kept as an option; the same metonymy as suscéptor → amparo. Ps 118:114 Vós sois o meu auxílio e o meu amparo: the Latinist asked for auxiliador … protetor three times — minor, then MAJOR twice with the text unchanged; refused each time (protetor is protéctor's; D19) — the final text stands against the gate there Ps 9:10, 9:35b auxílio … vós sereis auxílio para o órfão: the Latinist marked 9:10 MAJOR twice (drafts 2 and 3) for auxiliador; held on purpose (D24), his word is the option in Ps 9's decision adjutor. Ps 17:3b O meu Deus é o meu auxílio: the Latinist asked for auxiliador again — minor on draft 1, MAJOR on draft 2 with the words unchanged; held (D24), recorded in Ps 17's decision adjutor. Ps 17:2–3 sets four agent nouns side by side (liberátor, adjútor, protéctor, suscéptor): the Portuguese makes two agents (libertador, protetor) and two abstracts (auxílio, amparo). If Gustavo wants the series regular, Ps 17's decisions adjutor and susceptor turn both in one touch each. Ps 18:15b Dómine, adjútor meus, et redémptor meus → Senhor, meu auxílio, e meu redentor — HELD against four Latinist MAJORS (drafts 1–4), each asking meu auxiliador: here the agent noun redentor stands beside it and the metonymy is most exposed (77:35 has the same pair, Deus adjútor est eórum: et Deus excélsus redémptor eórum est). Held for D19 and the psalter's one word; needs a ruling — proposal: keep auxílio in the formula places (O Senhor é o meu auxílio) but allow auxiliador where adjútor is paired with another agent noun (18:15b, 77:35), or settle auxílio everywhere and record the gate's objection as D24 does. Ps 29:11 Dóminus factus est adjútor meus → o Senhor se fez o meu auxílio — HELD against two Latinist MAJORS (drafts 1 and 2) asking auxiliador, as 9:10 (se fez refúgio … auxílio), 17:19's build (se fez o meu protetor). No reader misheard it. Another place for the ruling this row asks. Ps 26:9b Adjútor meus esto → Sede o meu auxílio (a versicle, 05-01r.txt) — the Latinist's MAJOR auxiliador held again (D19/D24), as at 29:11. 27:7 (minor) the same. Ps 27:7 Dóminus adjútor meus … et adjútus sum → O Senhor é o meu auxílio … e fui auxiliado — the Latinist's remark (minor on draft 1, MAJOR on the gate) held (D19/D24). Ps 32:20 quóniam adjútor et protéctor noster est → porque o nosso auxílio e protetor é ele — HELD against two Latinist MAJORS (drafts 1 and 2) asking o nosso auxiliador e protetor. Here adjútor is paired with the agent noun protéctor — exactly the case this row's standing proposal names (with 18:15b, 77:35); add 32:20 to that list. auxiliador is option 2 in Ps 32's decision adjutor. Ps 39:18b Adjútor meus, et protéctor meus tu es → Vós sois o meu auxílio e o meu protetor (= 27:7's words; the pair with an agent noun again). The Latinist passed it twice this time; the stylist failed it ('an abstract noun beside a person-noun, the pair limps') and offered socorro / amparo — refused (socorro is as abstract; amparo is suscípere's, 39:12). 69:6b Adjútor meus, et liberátor meus es tu will copy the first half.

déxteraPs 15 Ps 16 Ps 19 Ps 20 Ps 25 Ps 117a direitaopenPs 117:16, Ps 90:7; MS1932 dextra / destra is the hieratic alternative Ps 17:36a e a vossa direita me amparou; the blind reader heard the right hand first. confúndiPs 30 Ps 33 Ps 34 Ps 39 Ps 118ser envergonhado; active não me envergonheissettled (D15)Ps 118:6, 31 (also 46, 78, 80, 116). Draft 1 had the traditional ser confundido; the blind reader heard "I shall not be confused" first in both verses, so it was changed. Costs for Gustavo to weigh: 30:2 = 70:1 non confúndar in ætérnum (the last line of the Te Deum) would not be the traditional não serei confundido; and erubéscere, which stands beside confúndi in 34:4/26, 39:15, 69:3–4, needs another word — proposed corar Ps 6:11 is the first erubéscere, and corar holds: Que todos os meus inimigos corem … e corem muito depressa — the Latin's own image (to redden), bare; the blind reader took it at once for shame, the stylist kept it twice in his own rebuilt line, the Latinist passed it. See the new row erubéscere. Ps 13:6 the active with men as subject, consílium ínopis confudístis → vós envergonhastes o conselho do indigente: D15's verb holds in the active; the Latinist passed it twice, the blind reader heard 'put to shame' without remark (he heard conselho as advice given). Ps 21:6 non sunt confúsi → não foram envergonhados (D15); heard as 'not humiliated'. Ps 24:3 não serão envergonhados, 24:4 Sejam envergonhados (beside erubéscere → corar in 24:1, 24:20). Ps 30:2 and 30:17 — the Te Deum line, decided here as D15 foresaw: In te, Dómine, sperávi, non confúndar in ætérnum → Em vós, Senhor, esperei: para sempre não serei envergonhado; 30:17 Dómine, non confúndar → Senhor, não serei envergonhado (one decision, confundar). confúndar is future and present subjunctive at once; the Greek has the optative (μὴ καταισχυνθείην) both times and DRB / MS1932 take the wish — the future is ruled, as 24:1 não corarei, with D23's formula for the negated in ætérnum. The blind reader heard 'never be put to shame'; the Latinist passed both places twice. The stylist asked não serei envergonhado para sempre (the 'not for ever' order, refused; option). Options: jamais, the wish não seja eu envergonhado, the traditional confundido. = 70:1 and the Te Deum's close (Common/Prayers.txt). Ps 34:4 Confundántur et revereántur → Sejam envergonhados e desonrados; 34:4b confundántur → sejam envergonhados; 34:26b confusióne → de vergonha. reveréri beside it needed a third word (new row).

declináre aPs 13 Ps 16 Ps 20 Ps 36 Ps 118apartar-se deopenPs 118:21 (also 51, 102, 157; 118:115 Apartai-vos de mim, malignos); keeps desviar for avértere Ps 13:3a Omnes declinavérunt (absolute; = 52:4 = Rom 3:12) → Todos se desviaram: without a, and next to juntos (simul), se apartaram risks 'they parted from one another'; the reflexive desviar-se is not avértere's transitive desviar. Transitive, declináre in + object: Ps 20:12 declinavérunt in te mala → voltaram males contra vós (the stylist's worst line was desviaram males contra vós); the twin 54:4 declinavérunt in me iniquitátes should follow (voltaram iniquidades contra mim). voltar is also convértere's — they do not meet in these lines. confirmárePs 36 Ps 37 Ps 40 Ps 118confirmaropenPs 118:28 confirmai-me nas vossas palavras; options fortalecer (MS1932), firmar Ps 36:17 confírmat autem justos Dóminus → draft 2 mas o Senhor sustenta os justos: draft 1 confirma os justos was heard by the blind reader as 'reconhece ou atesta que eles são justos'. consíliumPs 1 Ps 9 Ps 12 Ps 13 Ps 19 Ps 20 Ps 30 Ps 32 Ps 118conselho (advice; a deliberating body; where a verse can mean both) / desígnio (what someone intends: 9:23, 19:5, 20:12, 32:10–11) — D33settled (D33)Ps 1:1 (Ps 118:24 already has it); open between advice and a deliberating body, as the Latin is — the blind reader heard both Ps 12:2 Até quando hei de pôr conselhos na minha alma — the stylist's hei de pôr (a deliberative future) for draft 1's porei; the blind reader heard advice kept first, deliberation second — the Latin's range; planos (MS1932 projectos) is an option. Ps 19:5 omne consílium tuum → todo o teu desígnio — a local exception: a person's own plan, confirmed by God (the Latinist's MAJOR on todos os teus conselhos, and the blind reader heard advice given to others first). Ps 20:12 consília follows (desígnios). Proposal: conselho for advice / a council, desígnio for a plan. Ps 20:12 consília, quæ non potuérunt stabilíre → desígnios que não puderam firmar (Ps 19:5's word). Ps 32:10–11, the test D33 named: O Senhor dissipa os desígnios das nações … e rejeita os desígnios dos príncipes. * Mas o desígnio do Senhor permanece para sempre — the plans of nations, princes and God set against each other; the Latinist passed desígnio(s) on drafts 1 and 2, the stylist raised nothing. The blind reader listed desígnios and desígnio as unknown (as the Ps 9 reader did). The test holds for sense; for the ear it is evidence that planos (one touch away in Ps 32's decision consilia) may be wanted where the plan is not God's. Ps 9:23 comprehendúntur in consíliis quibus cógitant → ficam presos nos desígnios em que pensam (Ps 9 draft 8; D33, the build of 20:12; review 2026-09-21). 12:2 and 13:6 keep conselho under D33’s “where a verse can mean both”.
concíliumPs 1 Ps 21 Ps 25 Ps 39by its Greek (D34): congregação where συναγωγή (21:17); assembleia at 1:5 (βουλή, for the echo with 1:1 consílio); conselho at 25:4 (συνέδριον)settled (D34)Ps 1:5 in concílio justórum → na assembleia dos justos (from the stylist; DM1962's word). Draft 1 had the cognate concílio, for the Latin's echo consílio impiórum … concílio justórum (the Greek has βουλῇ twice): both blind readers stopped at it ("a council of bishops"; listed as unknown). Collision to rule on: assembleia is also the natural word for ecclésia, and Ps 21 has both (21:17 concílium malignántium, 21:23, 21:26 in ecclésia); other places 25:4, 39:11. congregação (MS1932) is congregátio's Ps 21 met the collision this row foretold, and rules it for review: two Latin words six verses apart, two Greek words (21:17 concílium malignántium = συναγωγὴ πονηρευομένων; 21:23, 21:26 ecclésia = ἐκκλησία), so two Portuguese words. ecclésia → assembleia everywhere (see the new row ecclésia); concílium here → a congregação dos malvados, on D15's test with synagóga → congregação (συναγωγή). The Greek behind concílium varies (read in the parallels files): 1:5 βουλή, 21:17 συναγωγή, 25:4 συνεδρίου, 39:11 συναγωγῆς. Proposal: concílium → congregação where it is συναγωγή (21:17, 39:11); 1:5 (βουλή) and 25:4 (συνέδριον) decide locally — 1:5's assembleia would then share the word with ecclésia in another psalm. Passed the Latinist twice; the blind reader heard 'a group of wicked people' (the cães as its image), never a religious order. Ps 25:4 Non sedi cum concílio vanitátis (συνεδρίου) → Não me sentei no conselho da vaidade — decided locally as D34 directs; draft 1 had com o conselho, and the blind reader heard advice (a recommendation dictated by vanity), so the preposition changed, not the word: one sits in a council. The gate Latinist (minor) wants com back for cum's company; refused, draft 1's words stay the option. Ps 39:11b a concílio multo → da congregação numerosa: checked — ἀπὸ συναγωγῆς πολλῆς (Rahlfs, in the parallels). D34's 'not yet checked' is closed. perírePs 9 Ps 118perecerworkingPs 1:6, Ps 2:12 Ps 118:176 sicut ovis, quæ périit cannot follow: ovelha que pereceu is a dead sheep, and the verse goes on quære servum tuum. Períre is both to perish and to be lost; perecer is only the first. There: como ovelha que se perdeu (DRB "a sheep that is lost"); passed every reader. Ps 9:37 períbitis, gentes, de terra illíus → perecereis da terra dele, nações: D20's fora de failed here — the Latinist (MAJOR) and the blind reader both heard 'die outside his land'. de is kept where 'die of' can make no sense, and the vocative moves last. So D20 is a rule for períre de via, not for every períre de.

nequándo … etPs 2 Ps 7 Ps 27 Ps 37para que não … e nãoworkingPs 2:12; eight places; the second não is supplied as Portuguese grammar needs (MS1932) Ps 27:1 nequándo táceas a me, et assimilábor descendéntibus in lacum → para que não vos caleis comigo, nem eu me torne semelhante aos que descem à cova — the second negation said as nem (the stylist: breath). Here the Latin's second verb is a future indicative (the consequence feared), and the Latinist made it a MAJOR (the negation and the subjunctive added; his fix não suceda que … e eu serei assemelhado): held — without the negation Portuguese hears the likeness wished for, and para que não / não suceda que govern the subjunctive. The same build returns in 142:7b (et símilis ero descendéntibus in lacum, after Ne avértas: there no nequándo, so the future can stand). After a verb of saying the quoted words are a wish: Que … não + subjunctive. para que não stays where the clause depends on a verb (2:12, 27:1). The gate Latinist asked nunca for the quando (minor, refused; option).

humílitas / humiliárePs 9 Ps 21 Ps 30 Ps 34 Ps 37 Ps 118humilhação / humilharopenPs 118:50, 67, 71, 75 (also 92, 107, 153; 9:14, 24:18 vide humilitátem meam); one family, as the psalm sets them together; humildade is heard as the virtue (but is the Magnificat's traditional word — unverified from memory); heard by the blind reader as being brought low first, affliction second Ps 21:22 et a córnibus unicórnium humilitátem meam → e dos chifres dos unicórnios a minha pequenez — a local departure: the object to be SAVED (a metonymy for the lowly self, like únicam meam); salvai … a minha humilhação would be heard as keeping the humiliation, and leão … humilhação rhymed at mediant and final. adoráre adPs 5 Ps 28adorar voltado paraopenPs 5:8b = 137:2a adorábo ad templum sanctum tuum → adorarei voltado para o vosso templo santo. The relation (προσκυνήσω πρός) kept against MS1932's no. History: em direção ao (draft 1; the Latinist's own wording, the stylist heard a route) → voltando-me para (draft 2; the Latinist: major, adds an action) → voltado para (draft 3, the stylist's; gate clean). Cost: a masculine participle the Latin does not have — whoever rules should weigh em direção ao, the genderless fall-back

mendácium, loqui mendáciumPs 5mentira, falar mentiraworkingPs 5:7a Ps 16:9b os eórum locútum est supérbiam → a boca deles falou soberba — the same accusative of content: draft 1 falou com soberba (DRB, MS1932: the manner) was marked MAJOR by the Latinist («A soberba é o conteúdo proferido»); taken on this row's precedent. 30:24, 100:7 fácere supérbiam are another build.

árguererepreender (não me repreendais no vosso furor)settled (D21)D21 overrules the agent’s acusar (kept as an option in Ps 6): every living witness says rebuke, and acusar makes God the prosecutor. The word is shared with increpáre; they never meet in a verse. The agent’s note: Ps 6:2 = 37:2; 5 verses (6:2, 37:2, 49:8, 49:21 árguam te, et státuam contra fáciem tuam, 93:10). L&S: with a person, "to accuse, reprove, censure, charge with" (arguisse accusasse et convicisse, Dig.); ἐλέγχω. Repreender (DRB rebuke, DM1962) is what the ear expects but is increpáre's word (ἐπιτιμάω, 12 verses), and repreendais has a hiatus at the stress; accusáre does not occur in the psalter; MS1932 arguir is an examiner's verb in Brazil. No reader objected. Gustavo's ear wanted: it opens two Penitential Psalms
corríperecastigar (nem me castigueis na vossa ira)settled (D21)Ps 6:2 = 37:2; 7 verses (38:12, 89:10, 93:10 Qui córripit gentes, non árguet?, 104:14, 140:5 Corrípiet me justus in misericórdia, et increpábit me). Shares the word with castigáre (117:18) on D15's test: one Greek verb (παιδεύω — checked for 117:18 in the parallels) and the two never meet (castigáre has no other verse). Corrigir is corrígere's (17:36b, 95:10, 118:9). MS1932, DRB chastise Ps 38:12 corripuísti hóminem → castigastes o homem, as this row names.
infírmusPs 6 Ps 9 Ps 17 Ps 25 Ps 30 Ps 40enfermo (porque estou enfermo)openPs 6:3, answered by sana me → curai-me; MS1932. The family mostly says weakness (infirmári 9:4, 25:1, 26:2, 108:24 génua mea infirmáta sunt → enfraquecer) but 102:3 sanat omnes infirmitátes tuas → enfermidades; DRB weak is the option. Masculine in the Latin too
sanárePs 6curar (curai-me, Senhor)openPs 6:3; 7 verses (29:3, 40:5 sana ánimam meam, 59:4, 102:3, 106:20, 146:3); the plain verb (DM1962), safe (past curei); MS1932 sarar is in Brazil mostly what the patient does Ps 29:3 clamávi ad te, et sanásti me → clamei a vós, * e me curastes — the row's verb; the stylist's best line; the blind reader heard bodily healing first, inner second.
valde / veheménterPs 6 Ps 20 Ps 30 Ps 118muito (both)openPs 6:4, 6:11. Draft 1 kept them apart (grandemente); the stylist found it heavy. Merged on D15's test — σφόδρα all three times in this psalm — and it lets 6:11 muito perturbados answer 6:4 muito perturbada. veheménter elsewhere (20:2, 46:10, 103:1, 104:24, 118:140, 167) may want grandemente locally Ps 118:167 diléxit ea veheménter → e os amou muito (held; short by three syllables). 118:140 Ignítum … veheménter → está todo em brasa: muito em brasa is not said; the Latinist (draft 16, minor) notes that todo is totality, not intensity — refused as the idiomatic intensive. Ps 20:2 exsultábit veheménter → exultará muito. Ps 30:12 et vicínis meis valde → e sobremaneira para os meus vizinhos — a local departure. Draft 1 e muito para os meus vizinhos was the stylist's worst line (muito hangs, not tied to afronta) and the blind reader could hear 'many neighbours'; draft 2 sobretudo (MS1932) was marked MAJOR by the Latinist gate (a comparison the Latin does not make) and his sobremaneira was taken verbatim (draft 3, unread). Where valde bears on a dative or stands at the end of a member, muito may need this help; grandemente is the option. Ps 30:12 → grandemente (Ps 30 draft 4, review 2026-09-21): sobremaneira (draft 3, the Latinist’s gate verbatim) was unknown to the blind reader; muito had been heard as “many neighbours”. A local departure from muito, as before.
úsquequoPs 4 Ps 12 Ps 118até quandoworkingPs 4:3, Ps 6:4 mas vós, Senhor, até quando? — the broken question left broken; 10 verses Ps 12:1–3: four Até quando — Úsquequo ×3 and 12:2 Quámdiu, merged (the Latin's anaphora is the psalm's shape; the stylist praised the repeated Até quando; Por quanto tempo for quámdiu is option 2). quámdiu elsewhere is "as long as" (103:33 quámdiu sum, 145:2a quámdiu fúero) and decides there.
inférnus / ínferiPs 6 Ps 9 Ps 13 Ps 15 Ps 17 Ps 29 Ps 30infernoopenPs 6:6 no inferno, porém, quem vos dará graças? — the Latin's word, kept (DRB hell). The blind reader heard the hell of the damned first, the world of the dead second. In 6:6 both are true; the hard tests are 15:10 non derelínques ánimam meam in inférno and 138:8 si descéndero in inférnum, ades; 19 verses. Options: os infernos (the old plural for the abode of the dead — the Creed's wording, from memory, unverified), a mansão dos mortos (the Creed as now said in Brazil — unverified; a paraphrase), MS1932 habitação dos mortos, DM1962 limbos. Heavy: wants a ruling Ps 9:18 Convertántur peccatóres in inférnum → Voltem os pecadores para o inferno: kept; here the damned's hell is no mishearing — the verse is a curse on the wicked; none of the three critics stopped at it. Ps 17:6 As dores do inferno me cercaram: kept (DRB the sorrows of hell); the blind reader heard the torments of the damned first, as foreseen — here the parallel laços da morte in the same verse points to the realm of the dead. For Gustavo with the rest of the row. Ps 15:10 — the hard test this row named: Quóniam non derelínques ánimam meam in inférno → Porque não abandonareis a minha alma no inferno: kept (DRB hell; MS1932 here no inferno). The Clementine of Acts, fetched (consult/bolls-VULG-44-2.json, -44-13.json), quotes the colon word for word (Acts 2:27) and applies it to Christ in 2:31 neque derelictus est in inferno, neque caro ejus vidit corruptionem; 13:35 quotes 15:10b. The blind reader heard, as foreseen, the place of punishment first, the realm of the dead second; the Latinist passed it twice; no critic asked for a change. Options in Ps 15: nos infernos, na mansão dos mortos, na morada dos mortos (the Creed's wordings still unverified). With 6:6, 9:18, 17:6 the row now has its four tests — ready for Gustavo's ruling. Ps 29:4 eduxísti ab inférno ánimam meam → tirastes do inferno a minha alma: kept (DRB hell; MS1932 inferno with a note, 'the grave'). The blind reader heard 'the place of eternal damnation' first, the world of the dead second — the fourth place so heard (after 6:6, 17:6, 15:10); here, as in 15:10, it is a soul brought back from it, where the damned's hell is the wrong sense. Passed the Latinist twice. mundo dos mortos is the option in Ps 29. Ps 30:18 deducántur in inférnum → sejam conduzidos ao inferno — a curse on the wicked, as 9:18; no reader stopped at it. dedúci in → ser conduzido (the passive of dúcere; D25's fazer descer has no sayable passive).

discédere aPs 118afastar-se de (Afastai-vos de mim)openPs 6:9 (DM1962 word for word); the imperative only here; stays apart from declináre a → apartar-se de (118:115, a near twin); shares the verb with the transitive amovére / repéllere → afastar. Ps 118:118 omnes discedéntes a judíciis tuis → todos os que se afastam dos vossos juízos (reached independently) Ps 21:11 ne discésseris a me → não vos afasteis de mim — which is why 21:20 ne elongáveris … a me (another verb, μακρύνω) is não ponhais longe de mim (see the new row elongáre). suscípere (a thing as object)Ps 118acolher (o Senhor acolheu a minha oração)openPs 6:10. D19's amparar was made on persons; with a prayer (6:10), mercy (47:10 Suscépimus, Deus, misericórdiam tuam) or peace (71:3 Suscípiant montes pacem) as object it fails, and the Greek changes verb too (προσδέχομαι in 6:10). Acolher is the nearest neighbour of amparar; receber (MS1932, DRB) is the option. 16:12 Suscepérunt me sicut leo (hostile) will need a third Ps 16:12, the hostile place this row foresaw: Suscepérunt me sicut leo (ὑπέλαβόν με) → Agarraram-me como um leão — a third verb, free in the glossary (tomar is accípere's, 17:17; arrebatar rápere's; prender comprehéndere's). No reader remarked. Options Apanharam-me, Tomaram-me (DRB).
erubéscerecoraropenPs 6:11, twice; D15's proposal tried and it holds (see confúndi); 9 verses; 82:18 Erubéscant … et confundántur → Corem … e sejam envergonhados. Options corar de vergonha, envergonhar-se (differs from confúndi only in voice) Ps 24:1 = 24:20 non erubéscam → não corarei (the future: confidence; the Greek's optative μὴ καταισχυνθείην is option que eu não core), beside confúndi → ser envergonhado in 24:3–4. exspectáre / exspectátioPs 26 Ps 38 Ps 39 Ps 118aguardar / o que aguardo (Os pecadores me aguardaram; não me envergonheis pelo que aguardo)settled (D39); was: settled (D24)Ps 118:95, 116; kept apart from speráre → esperar em, spes → esperança, supersperáre (the Greek keeps them apart too: ὑπομένω, προσδοκία). The noun: expectativa failed the stylist ("bureaucratic"), no que aguardo failed him too (the preposition), pelo que aguardo stands; the Latinist (minor) wants the noun, pela minha expectativa. 14 verses: 26:14 Exspécta Dóminum, 36:34, 38:8, 39:2 Exspéctans exspectávi Dóminum, 51:11, 54:9, 68:7, 68:21, 103:11, 103:27, 118:166 Exspectábam salutáre tuum, 141:8 — ruled: D24 (aguardar), D36 (the three verbs kept apart) Ps 24 proposes sustinére (God as object) → esperar por (todos os que esperam por vós, 24:3; por vós esperei, 24:5, 24:21), the third verb beside esperar em (speráre) and aguardar (this row); 26:14 Exspécta Dóminum … et sústine Dóminum will test the three. The blind reader heard 'waiting for God to act'. Ps 26:14 Exspécta Dóminum … et sústine Dóminum → Aguarda o Senhor … e espera pelo Senhor — HANDOFF Open 14 decided with the three verbs kept apart (speráre in → esperar em, 26:3b nisto eu esperarei); the Greek has ὑπόμεινον twice, the Latin varies and the variation costs nothing; no reader objected. For Gustavo's review. Ps 38:8 quæ est exspectátio mea? Nonne Dóminus? → qual é a minha espera? Não é o Senhor? — draft 1 had the row's clause (que é que eu aguardo?); the Latinist (minor) and the stylist ('filler') both asked for the noun espera; taken. Proposal for the row: espera is the plain noun it lacked (waiting, not hope) where exspectátio stands as subject or predicate; pelo que aguardo stays for 118:116's ab exspectatióne. Cost: espera shares the root of esperar (D36's cost). Greek here ὑπομονή. Ps 39:2 Exspéctans exspectávi Dóminum → Aguardando, aguardei o Senhor (gerund + verb, the participle row; D39); passed all three readers, the stylist named it among the best.

odísse / ódio habérePs 5 Ps 30 Ps 37odiar / ter ódio a, object first (Aos iníquos tive ódio)openPs 118:104 odívi → odiei; 118:113, 128 (163 to come) ódio hábui. Draft 7 merged them (one Greek verb, ἐμίσησα); the stylist then faulted the proparoxytone cadences iníquos / iníquo and himself proposed Aos iníquos tive ódio — the Latin word for word — so the periphrasis came back for the ear's sake. 24:19, 138:22 ódio iníquo / perfécto óderam are another build Ps 24:19 ódio iníquo odérunt me → e com ódio iníquo me odeiam — the Latinist's MAJOR on odiaram: odérunt is a perfect with present sense (as 17:18 que me odeiam); the late perfect odívi (118:104 odiei) is another form. Ps 25:5 Odívi → Odeio — the Latinist's MAJOR on draft 1's Odiei, taken: odi is a perfect with present sense (24:19 odérunt → me odeiam, the same ruling). 118:104 odiei should be re-read by it. Ps 30:7 Odísti (God) → Vós odiais — the present sense (24:19, 25:5); passed the Latinist twice. 118:104 odívi → odeio (Ps 118 draft 18, review 2026-09-21). Which contexts take which tense: odi / odívi, odérunt, odísti are perfects of a state and take the present (24:19, 25:5, 30:7, 118:104); ódio hábui keeps the past periphrasis (118:113, 128) or Odiei (118:163); a past narrative keeps the past (35:5 malítiam autem non odívit → não odiou a malícia, beside meditátus est … ástitit).

fácere judíciumPs 1 Ps 9 Ps 16 Ps 24 Ps 32 Ps 35 Ps 36 Ps 118fazer juízo sobre (118:84) / praticar o juízo (118:121)opentwo builds: 118:84 quando fareis juízo sobre os que me perseguem?
